## Document 004: 10月会合で日銀は政策金利を据え置き 2％の物価安定に向け利上げ継続へ

### Page 1

日本

株式会社大和総研 丸の内オフィス 〒100-6756 東京都千代田区丸の内一丁目 9番 1号 グラントウキョウ ノースタワー

性を保証するものではありません。また、記載された意見や予測等は作成時点のものであり今後予告なく変更されることがあります。㈱大和総研と大和証券㈱は、㈱大和
証券グループ本社を親会社とする大和証券グループの会社です。内容に関する一切の権利は㈱大和総研にあります。無断での複製・転載・転送等はご遠慮ください。

2026年 10月 30日 全5頁

10月会合で日銀は政策金利を据え置き 
2％の物価安定に向け利上げ継続へ 
利上げの悪影響は今のところ限定的、12月に1.5％へ追加利上げか

経済調査部 チーフエコノミスト 神田 慶司 
  エコノミスト 秋元 虹輝

[要約] 
◼ 日本銀行（日銀）は2026年10月29、30日の金融政策決定会合で、短期金利を1.25％
程度に据え置いた。日銀の政策運営は、基調的な物価上昇率を「2％に近づける」局面
から「2％程度で安定させる」局面に移った。今後は、経済・物価・金融情勢に応じて
政策金利を引き上げ、金融緩和の度合いを調整していく方針だ。 
◼ 日銀は2024年 3月以降、金融政策の正常化を進めてきたが、企業・家計への悪影響は
これまでのところ限定的だ。企業では比較的良好な資金調達環境やネット有利子負債
比率の低下が、家計では収入の増加や借入返済額の減少に加え、住宅ローン金利上昇
の返済額への反映の遅れが、足元の負担増を抑えている。ただし、家計の借入残高は
収入対比で高水準にあり、金利高が長期化すれば、返済負担が徐々に高まる可能性が
ある。 
◼ 当社では、日銀は 2026年 12月会合で短期金利を1.5％、2027年 3月会合で 1.75％へ
引き上げた後、いったん据え置くとみている。ただし、その後も金融環境がなお緩和
的で物価上振れリスクが残れば、同年6月会合で2.0％への追加利上げに踏み切る可能
性がある。

2026年 10月会合で日銀は政策金利の据え置きを決定 
日本銀行（日銀）は2026年10月29、30日に開催された金融政策決定会合で、無担保コール
レート・オーバーナイト物（以下、短期金利）を 1.25％程度に据え置くことを決めた1。今回
は利上げを決定した翌月の会合ということもあり、これまでの利上げの影響を見極めることを
重視したとみられる。

1 日本銀行「当面の金融政策運営について」（2026年10月 30日）

### Page 2

1

2 / 5

連続利上げに慎重な姿勢は、前回会合後の総裁発言からもうかがえる。9月会合直後の記者
会見で植田和男総裁は、「大きな幅の利上げとか何回も連続して利上げするというようなこと
は、端的に申し上げれば、物価が目標を超えてものすごい上がっていくというリスクが高いか、
あるいは既に目標を超えちゃっているかというようなことに依存するんだと思います」2と述べ、
その例として2022、23年の米欧の高インフレ局面を挙げた。 
また、日銀が保有する上場投資信託（ETF）については、2026年 1月以降の売却が市場に大
きな影響を与えることなく円滑に進捗したことなどを踏まえ、2027年 1月から年間の売却ペー
スを簿価で 3,300億円程度から 8,600億円程度（市場全体の売買代金に占める年間売却額（時
価）の割合は0.05％程度から0.1％程度）に引き上げることを決めた3。

インフレ基調は2％目標と概ね整合的で、今後は定着に向けて利上げ継続へ 
9月会合の声明文で「2％に近づいている」4と評価した消費者物価の基調的な上昇率につい
ては、10月会合で公表された「経済・物価情勢の展望」（展望レポート）で「2％の『物価安定
の目標』と概ね整合的な水準」に修正された。さらに、「従来は、基調的な物価上昇率を 2％に
向けて引き上げていく過程にあり、物価の下振れリスクに相応の注意を払うことが必要であっ
たが、足もとでは局面が変化しており、今後は、基調的な物価上昇率が 2％を超えて上振れて
いくリスクが顕在化し、それがその後の経済に悪影響を及ぼすことがないよう、十分に留意す
る必要がある」と指摘した5。 
植田総裁は 9月会合直後の記者会見ですでに、基調的な物価上昇率を 2％程度で安定させて
いくことが重要になったとして、 「政策の局面が変化した」と説明していた。10月会合での文
言変更は、こうした政策局面の変化をより明確にしたものといえる。また、10月 16日の記者
会見でも、今後は基調的な物価上昇率が「これ以上上がるということは容認できない」6と述べ、
2％近辺での定着を目指す考えを改めて示した。 
展望レポートでは、引き続き物価上振れリスクへの警戒を示し、経済・物価・金融情勢に応
じて政策金利を引き上げ、金融緩和の度合いを調整していく方針を前回と同様に明記した。日
銀が物価上振れリスクを見極める上で注視しているのが、①中東情勢に伴う原油価格、②AI関
連需要、③為替相場の3つである。足元ではAI関連投資の収益性への懸念が広がり、円安も一
服しているものの、日銀はこれらの動向が国内物価にどの程度波及するかを引き続き見極めて
いくとみられる。 
展望レポートを見ると、2026年度の実質 GDP成長率は春先以降のエネルギー・原材料価格の
上昇を受けて伸び率を縮小しつつも緩やかな成長を続け、2027年度以降は所得から支出への前

2 日本銀行「総裁記者会見」（2026年9月18日、24日公表） 
3 日本銀行「ETFの売却ペースの見直しに関する決定（2026年 10月）」（2026年 10月30日） 
4 日本銀行「金融市場調節方針の変更について」（2026年9月 18日） 
5 日本銀行「経済・物価情勢の展望（2026年10月）」（基本的見解、2026年10月 30日） 
6 日本銀行「植田総裁記者会見(10月 16日) ―G20終了後の植田総裁、三村財務官 共同記者会見における総裁
発言」（2026年10月 20日）

### Page 3

1

3 / 5

向きな循環メカニズムが強まるもとで成長率を緩やかに高めていくとされた（政策委員見通し
の中央値は2026年度＋0.7％、27年度＋0.7％、28年度＋0.8％） 。 
一方、消費者物価（除く生鮮食品）の前年比は、原油価格の上昇や半導体価格等の上昇、為
替円安などを背景に見通し期間前半に 2％をはっきりと上回る水準まで伸び率を高めた後、後
半には原油高の影響の減衰により、 2％程度に向けてプラス幅を縮小する見通しだ（政策委員
見通しの中央値は 2026年度＋2.5％、2027年度＋2.6％、2028年度＋2.0％） 。夏場以降の原油
価格上昇の影響などから、2027年度が前回（7月）から 0.2％pt上方修正された。消費者物価
（除く生鮮食品・エネルギー）の見通しは 2026年度＋2.4％、2027年度＋2.7％、2028年度＋
2.3％と、前回からの修正は小幅にとどまった。なお、2027年 4月から実施される可能性のあ
る飲食料品の消費税率引き下げは、物価見通しに反映されていない。 
利上げが続く中でも企業・家計への影響はこれまでのところ限定的 
日銀は2024年3月の金融政策決定会合でマイナス金利政策の解除とイールドカーブ・コント
ロール（長短金利操作）の撤廃を決め、その後 5回の利上げや国債買入れの減額を実施するな
ど金融政策の正常化を進めてきた。 
もっとも、企業や家計の経済活動への悪影響はこれまでのところ限定的で、金融環境はなお
緩和的とみられる。まず企業部門を見ると、日銀短観の業況判断DIは高水準を維持しており、
資金繰り判断DIや金融機関の貸出態度判断DIにも目立った悪化は見られない（図表1左）。中
小企業でも顕著な悪化は見られず、利上げが進む中でも、企業の景況感や資金調達環境は総じ
て良好な状態にある。 
ただし、過去の利上げ局面との単純な比較には注意が必要だ。1990年頃までの利上げは、資
産バブル期を含む高成長・高インフレ環境の下で実施されたのに対し、 2000年代以降は低成
長・デフレないし低インフレが長く続き、企業の財務構造も大きく変化した。足元で利上げの
影響が表れにくい背景には、企業の財務体質の改善がある。 
有利子負債から現預金を控除した額の総資産に対する比率（ネット有利子負債比率）は、資
本金 1千万円以上 10億円未満の中小・中堅企業で歴史的な低水準にある（図表 1右）。大企業
では中小・中堅企業ほど低下しておらず、資本金 1千万円未満の企業では相対的に高いものの、
企業部門全体では 1970～90 年代に比べて大幅に低下している。こうした財務構造の変化によ
り、政策金利が上昇しても、企業部門全体では利払い費の増加を通じた金融引き締めの影響が
以前より表れにくくなっていると考えられる。

図表 1：日銀短観の各種 DIの長期推移（左） 、企業規模別に見たネット有利子負債比率（右）

（注）左図は全産業ベースで、破線で示した中小企業は資本金 2千万円以上 1億円未満。 「利上げ局面」は利上
げ開始から利下げ直前までの期間。右図のネット有利子負債比率は、有利子負債から現預金を控除して総資産
で除したもの。資本金 1千万円未満のデータは年度値の平均（その他は四半期値の平均） 。 
（出所）日本銀行、財務省より大和総研作成

家計部門についても、これまでのところ金利上昇の影響は限定的だ。ただし、ストック面か
ら金利上昇への耐性を見ると、借入残高対年収比率は 2000年代初めから大幅に上昇している
（図表 2左）。データが遡及可能な2002年と比べると、2026年上半期の同比率は約53％pt高い。
要因別では、借入残高の増加が同比率を約 66％pt押し上げた一方、年収の増加が約 12％pt押
し下げた。家計の借入負担はストック面で以前より重くなっており、金利上昇が長期化すれば、
その影響が徐々に顕在化する可能性がある。 
図表 2：家計の借入残高対年収比率（左） 、借入返済負担率（右）の2002年からの変化幅

（注）二人以上の勤労者世帯。左図の借入残高対年収比率は、1 世帯あたりの借入残高（「住宅・土地のための
負債」と「住宅・土地以外の負債」の合計）を年間世帯収入で除したもの。右図の借入返済負担率は 1世帯あ
たりの実収入に対する借金返済額の比率。「低所得世帯」は年間収入五分位階級のうち第Ⅰ・Ⅱ分位、「高所得
世帯」は第Ⅳ・Ⅴ分位を集計。「返済世帯比率要因」は、住宅ローン返済世帯の収入が二人以上の勤労者世帯
全体の収入に占める割合の変化による寄与を表す。 
（出所）総務省より大和総研作成

### Page 5

1

5 / 5

一方、フロー面から足元の資金繰りを見ると、収入に対する借入返済額の比率（借入返済負
担率）は、返済額の減少や収入の増加を背景に、2002年以降の低水準にある（図表2右）。2026
年上半期の借入返済負担率は2002年を約1.1％pt下回った。要因別では、住宅ローン返済世帯
の収入シェア（住宅ローン返済世帯の収入が二人以上の勤労者世帯全体の収入に占める割合）
の上昇が負担率を押し上げた一方、返済額の減少と収入の増加が押し下げた。所得階級別に見
ると、足元では低所得世帯（年収下位 4割）の返済負担率が相対的に上昇しているものの、低
所得世帯、高所得世帯（年収上位4割）ともに2002年の水準を下回る。このため、金利上昇が
家計の資金繰りを大きく圧迫する状況には至っていない。 
ただし、足元の返済負担の低さは今後も続くとは限らない。変動金利型住宅ローンでは、毎
月の返済額の急増を抑える「5 年ルール」や「125％ルール」が広く採用されている7。こうし
た仕組みは、金利上昇の影響が返済額に反映される時期を遅らせ、短期的な負担増を和らげる
半面、負担の顕在化を将来に先送りする。借入残高が高水準にあることも踏まえると、金利高
が長期化するほど家計の返済負担が高まる可能性には留意が必要だ。

中立金利を探りながら、日銀は段階的な利上げを継続へ 
当社では、日銀は次回 2026年 12月の金融政策決定会合で短期金利を1.5％に、2027年 3月
会合で 1.75％に引き上げるとみている。その後は経済・物価・金融情勢への影響を見極めるた
め、いったん政策金利を据え置くと予想する。ただし、1.75％への引き上げ後も金融環境がな
お緩和的で、物価上振れリスクが残る場合には、同年 6月会合で 2.0％への追加利上げに踏み
切る可能性がある。 
日銀は2026年3月に公表したレポート8で、6つのモデルによる自然利子率の推計レンジを▲
0.9％程度～＋0.5％程度と示した。2％の物価上昇率を想定すると、名目の中立金利は 1.1～
2.5％程度で、1.75％はそのレンジのほぼ中央に位置する。だが、中立金利を正確に推計する
ことは困難で、その推計レンジには大きな幅がある9。そのため日銀は、各種データでこれまで
の利上げの影響や物価の基調などを確認しながら、金融緩和の度合いを徐々に調整していくと
みられる。

7 5年ルールは、変動金利型住宅ローンの金利が変動しても、毎月の返済額を5年間据え置く仕組み。125％ル
ールは、返済額の見直し時に、新たな返済額の上限を従来の 125％とする仕組み。いずれも法令で一律に定め
られたものではなく、適用の有無は金融機関や商品によって異なる。 
8 日本銀行「自然利子率の動向と金融緩和度合いの評価」（「日銀レビュー」、2026-J-5、2026年3月） 
9 2026年6月会合後の記者会見で内田眞一副総裁は、中立金利の推計値には「相当なばらつき」があるとした
上で、「短期金利を上げていく過程で、それを含めた金融環境の変化ということを点検して、中立金利の水準
を探りながら、金融緩和の度合いを調整していく」と述べた（日本銀行「総裁記者会見（内田副総裁代理）」
（2026年6月16日、17日公表））。

## Document 040: 20261027【投研報告】市場快訊_MSCI降評風險升高，印尼股市保守看待

不另行通知。
MSCI 降評風險升高，印尼股市保守看待
2026 年 10 月 27 日
一、 市場動態
1. MSCI 擬將印尼降至獨立市場，降評風險明顯升高：MSCI 於 10 月 15 日宣布，啟動
將印尼股市由「新興市場（Emerging Market）」直接降至「獨立市場（Standalone
Market） 」的諮詢程序，意見徵詢將於2027 年 4 月 30 日截止，最晚於 2027 年 6 月
30 日公布最終決定 。MSCI 原先於今年 1 月提出可能降至 「邊境市場 （Frontier Market
） 」 ，此次則跳過「邊境市場」提出直接將該國股市降至「獨立市場」的方案，顯示對

印尼市場治理的疑慮進一步加深。雖然「邊境市場」仍可能是替代分類方案，但MSCI
強調，除非有明確證據顯示市場治理及運作出現實質改善，否則預期將依目前提案重
新分類，顯示此次降評風險較過去顯著升高。
2. 股權透明度與交易秩序問題為 MSCI 主要疑慮：MSCI 指出，印尼股市長期存在股權
結構不透明、實際自由流通股比例難以確認、疑似協調交易，以及市場監理執法成效
不足等問題，影響國際投資人對市場公平性與可投資性的信心。雖然印尼主管機關已
提出強化持股超過 1%股東資訊揭露、建立高持股集中度（HSC）監理機制，以及規
劃逐步將最低自由流通股比例提高至 15%等改革措施，但MSCI 認為相關改革尚未展
現持續且具實質性的改善成果。由於問題涉及市場制度與治理結構，改善所需時間可
能較一般短期市場波動更長。
3. 股市反彈後再度回落，外資撤離壓力仍高：受降評消息衝擊，印尼雅加達綜合指數 10
月 16 日重挫 11.57%，創 1998 年以來最大單日跌幅，19 日再跌 3.64%，盤中最低
觸及 4,907.37 點 。 雖然20 日至 23 日連續四個交易日反彈 ， 但26 日再度下跌 1.22%

，收於 5,373.09 點，各產業類股全面收黑，顯示市場信心仍不穩定。根據 Reuters
報導，截至 10 月 19 日，2026 年外資已累計淨賣超印尼股票約 48.8 億美元。此外，
印尼證交所 26 日宣布，將引進獨立專家及外國投資人代表參與高持股集中度審查，
並自 11 月起加強調查疑似協調交易，必要時凍結可疑帳戶，以回應 MSCI 對市場制
度的疑慮。
二、 下跌原因分析：
1. 股市：潛在指數剔除與資金外流，持續壓抑市場評價
若印尼股市最終降至「獨立市場」， 原則上將不再納入MSCI 新興市場及邊境市場相關
指數，追蹤相關指數的被動式基金將須依規則調整持股，實際處理方式仍以 MSCI 後
續公告為準。若改降至「邊境市場」，則可能獲得部分邊境市場資金承接，衝擊相對較
小。Reuters 引述多家金融機構估計，印尼股市降至獨立市場可能引發約 30 億至 60

### Page 2

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
億美元被動資金流出，其中 UBS 估計約 40 億美元，並指出主動型基金另有約 150 億
美元潛在撤資風險，惟實際規模仍視最終分類結果及基金策略而定。
2. 匯率：外資撤離增加印尼盾貶值壓力
印尼股市的降評風險不僅衝擊股市，也可能透過外資撤離增加印尼盾貶值壓力。印尼
盾 10 月 16 日盤中一度貶至 18,070 兌 1 美元，為 7 月下旬以來最弱水準之一，當日
收盤約 18,000。若後續國際資金持續流出，可能進一步增加股匯市波動，對以外幣計
價、且未完全避險的印尼基金及 ETF 形成額外壓力。雖然印尼央行可透過匯市干預及

流動性管理措施穩定金融市場，但在資金外流壓力尚未緩解前，印尼盾仍可能維持偏
弱格局。
3. 基金與 ETF：指數權重下滑，單一市場曝險風險升高
MSCI 相關指數調整已對印尼股票產生實質影響。Reuters 引用 LSEG 資料顯示，印尼
在 MSCI 新興市場指數的權重已由 2026 年初的 1.16%降至 9 月底的 0.43%，相關成
分股亦由年初 18 檔減至 9 檔，反映部分指數資金調整壓力已提前出現。對印尼單一
國家基金及 ETF 而言，主要風險來自股市下跌、資金流出及印尼盾波動；主動式基金
雖可透過選股降低部分影響，但仍難以完全迴避整體市場風險。不同產品實際受到的
影響，仍應依追蹤指數、主要持股及匯率避險安排個別評估。
三、 市場看法：
1. 制度性問題難以速解，降評風險將持續牽動市場：我們認為，此次 MSCI 降評風險並
非單純反映經濟成長放緩或企業獲利不佳，而是涉及市場透明度、交易公平性與監理
執法等結構性問題。尤其 MSCI 不僅啟動降評諮詢，更持續限制印尼股票的部分指數

調整，包括不提高外資納入因子、不新增相關成分股，以及不進行向上規模分類調整
，顯示市場可投資性疑慮尚未獲得有效解決。儘管政府試圖推動改革，但制度修正仍
須經過執行與成效驗證，才能重新取得國際投資人的信任，因此短期內降評風險仍難
完全消除。
2. 外資撤離與指數權重下降，市場信心修復恐需較長時間：印尼今年以來外資已大幅賣
超，MSCI 新興市場指數中的權重亦明顯下降，顯示國際投資人對當地市場的疑慮已
逐漸反映於實際資金流向。我們認為，即使印尼最終成功避免降評，部分國際機構投
資人仍可能因市場透明度、流動性及治理品質疑慮而維持較低曝險。此外，MSCI 對
部分指數調整的凍結措施尚未解除，短期內新增指數資金流入機會仍受到限制，預期
國際資金回流與市場評價修復均可能需要較長時間。
3. 監理改革力度加大，但尚不足以扭轉保守展望：印尼政府近期持續加強改革，包括提
高資訊透明度、強化交易監控、對違規交易開罰，以及引進外部獨立人士參與監理審
查。10 月 26 日該國政府進一步提出主動調查疑似協調交易及凍結可疑帳戶等措施，

11 月 2 日起亦規劃將高持股集中度股票排除於雅加達綜合指數之外 。 相關改革有助改

### Page 3

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
善交易秩序及短期市場信心，但部分措施仍待正式實施，且實際成效尚未獲得 MSCI
認可。考量制度性問題改善需要時間，政策利多雖可能帶動短線反彈，但尚不足以改
變對印尼股市中期前景的保守判斷。
4. 股市跌深不代表利空出盡，中期風險報酬吸引力偏低：印尼股市在 10 月 16 日及 19
日大幅修正後，雖曾連續四個交易日反彈，但 26 日再度回落，顯示市場尚未形成穩
定回升趨勢。我們認為，當前股市面臨的不僅是短期賣壓，更包括國際投資人信任下
降及風險溢價提高，即使企業基本面未同步惡化，仍可能因評價下修而承受股價壓力

。因此，短線不排除政策利多或跌深後出現技術性反彈，但在 MSCI 態度、外資流向
與印尼盾走勢明顯改善前，反彈延續性仍有疑慮，印尼股市中期展望維持保守。
四、市場展望：
1. 印尼股市維持保守展望，單一市場風險報酬吸引力不足：考量 MSCI 降評風險尚未解除、
外資撤離壓力持續 ， 以及市場治理改革仍有待驗證 ， 我們認為印尼股市未來數季的風險報
酬吸引力相對有限。即使近期股價明顯回落，仍難認定相關風險已充分反映。相較市場制
度成熟、國際資金流動性較佳的新興市場，印尼股市可能持續面臨較高風險溢價，因此對
單一印尼市場的集中曝險應維持審慎態度。
2. 此事件對新興市場指數與東協指數影響相對有限：根據 MSCI 資料，截至2026 年 9 月底
，印尼股市在 MSCI Emerging Market 指數與 MSCI AC ASEAN 指數中的權重分別約為
0.43%與 7.55%，因此我們認為印尼潛在降評對相關指數的直接衝擊相對有限。此外，新
興市場各國經濟結構與產業組成有所差異 ， 區域型基金可透過跨國分散降低單一市場風險

。惟後續仍須留意印尼事件對整體新興市場投資信心造成的外溢影響 ， 以及全球貿易與匯
率波動風險。

   公司簡介 
華邦電子是利基型記憶體與積體電路解決方案供應商，核心產品包括 DRAM、NOR 
Flash、NAND Flash 以及邏輯 IC 產品，廣泛應用於手持裝置、消費性電子、電腦周

➢ 3Q26 報價漲幅強勁，惟位元出貨量下滑 
3Q26 營收 823.3 億元，QoQ+37.58%，YoY+278.18%，成長動能主要
仍來自於記憶體短缺所帶來的報價強勁漲幅，惟公司為因應 LTA 需求而
實行嚴格控貨策略，導致出貨量呈現下滑。獲利表現方面，3Q26 毛利率
約 70.57%，QoQ+4.32ppts，YoY+23.88ppts， 稅後淨利354.22 億元 ，
QoQ+45.67%，YoY+1,103.54%，EPS 為 7.58 元。  
➢ 4Q26 Flash 產品仍具強勁上漲動能，獲利表現可望持續提升 
展望 4Q26，預估 DRAM 產品 ASP 漲幅約逾 10%，NOR 產品漲幅可望
逾 20%。 預估4Q26 營收約 994.8 億元 ，QoQ+20.83%，YoY+273.64%，
毛利率 72.25%， 稅後淨利416 億元 ，QoQ+17.44%，YoY+1,115.55%，
EPS 為 8.81 元。  
➢ 2027 年 DRAM 及 Flash 新產能開出，挹注營收成長動能 
公司目標將高雄廠產能擴增至 24K，隨新產能於 2H26 投片，且新增產
能皆將採用 16nm 製程，逐步取代舊有的 25Snm 與部分 20nm 製程，
位元出貨量預估將會大幅增加。考量記憶體供需吃緊帶動報價漲幅優於
預期 ， 且隨著位元出貨量逐季成長 ， 投研部預估2026 年全年營收約 2799
億元，毛利率 67.89%，稅後淨利 1114.5 億元，EPS 為 24.04 元。展望
2027 年，預期全球市場將面臨更為緊俏且吃緊的供需格局，而受惠於約
5K 的產能擴充，Flash 2027 年的位元出貨量成長率可望突破 40%。
DRAM 方面 ， 高雄廠則預計滿載月產能將達24~25K， 並透過製程轉換 ，
提高 16nm 製程比重，進一步推成位元出貨量。投研部預估 2027 年營
收約 6,008 億 元 ， 毛 利 率71.21% ， 稅 後 淨 利 為2,550 億元，
YoY+128.84%，EPS 為 54.03 元。  
➢ CUBE 及矽電容陸續貢獻營收，中長期營運動能正向 
評價方面，考量公司 3Q26 ASP 漲勢優於先前預期，且 4Q26 Flash 產
品仍具上漲空間，再加上 2027 年位元出貨量可望迎來顯著成長，營收
成長動能強勁之餘，中長期亦有 CUBE 以及矽電容營收貢獻，預期營運
至 2028 年可望維持高成長態勢，故投研部維持強力買進建議，目標價
205 元(2027F PE 4X)。

個股報告

公司簡介 
華邦電子股份有限公司成立於 1987 年 9 月，公司在中科與南科高雄園區各設有一座
12 吋晶圓廠 。華邦電子是利基型記憶體與積體電路解決方案供應商 ， 同時擁有自有產
品及技術，從產品設計、研發、晶圓製造到品牌行銷完整垂直整合。核心產品包括
DRAM、NOR Flash、NAND Flash 以及邏輯 IC 產品，廣泛應用於手持裝置、消費性
電子 、 電腦周邊 、 車用與工業電子等市場 。2026 年產品組合為 CMS53%、Flash 35%、
Logic 11%。

資料來源：公司、國票投顧預估 
3Q26 報價漲幅強勁，惟位元出貨量下滑 
3Q26 營收 823.3 億元，QoQ+37.58%，YoY+278.18%，營收成長動能主要仍來自
於記憶體短缺所帶來的報價強勁漲幅 ，惟營收仍低於先前預期 ， 主要因公司為因應 LTA
需求而策略性保留部分產能，實行嚴格控貨策略，導致產能利用率雖然滿載，出貨量
呈現下滑。產品報價漲幅方面皆優於預期，DRAM 產品 ASP 約季增 60%，Flash 產
品 ASP 則季增逾 30%；然而在位元出貨量方面，DRAM 出貨量約季減 8%，Flash 產
品則受惠於高容量 SLC/NOR 需求維持強勁，產能利用率滿載，出貨量與 2Q26 大致
持平。獲利表現方面，3Q26 毛利率約 70.57%，QoQ+4.32ppts，YoY+23.88ppts，
稅後淨利 354.22 億元，QoQ+45.67%，YoY+1,103.54%，EPS 為 7.58 元。 
4Q26 Flash 產品仍具強勁上漲動能，獲利表現可望持續提升 
展望 4Q26，雖然面臨消費性電子產品淡季修正，部分客戶在 4Q26 進入庫存調節，
然而 AI 伺服器、車用與 Edge AI 等領域的拉貨動能依然保持強勁。預估 DRAM 產品
ASP 漲幅約逾 10%，NOR 產品則受惠於 AI 伺服器對 512Mb 以上高容量 NOR Flash
的大量需求，短期缺口難以填補，市場報價仍具備繼續上行的空間，預估 ASP 漲幅仍
可望逾 20%。位元出貨量方面，預期 DRAM 可望受惠製程升級以及良率提升，出貨
量下滑幅度可望收斂，Flash 產品則可望在台中廠產能將針對 24 奈米進行小量擴產，
帶動位元出 貨量逐季 提升。預估 4Q26 營收約 994.8 億 元，QoQ+20.83% ，
YoY+273.64%，毛利率 72.25%，QoQ+1.68ppts，YoY+30.38ppts，稅後淨利 416
億元，QoQ+17.44%，YoY+1,115.55%，EPS 為 8.81 元。

個股報告 
2027 年 DRAM 及 Flash 新產能開出，挹注營收成長動能 
目前高雄廠月產能約為 15K，公司目標在年底前將 16nm 機台買足至 16K 的投片規
模，並計畫在年底前將高雄廠產能擴增至 24K，隨著新產能於 2H26 開始投片，且新
增產能皆將採用 16nm 製程，逐步取代舊有的 25Snm 與部分 20nm 製程，位元出貨
量預估將會大幅增加，預估 2H26 僅少量 16nm 產品將貢獻營收，預計 2027 年營收
貢獻將大幅提升。考量記憶體供需吃緊帶動報價漲幅優於預期，且隨著位元出貨量逐
季成長，投研部預估 2026 年全年營收約 2,799 億元，YoY+213%，毛利率 67.89%，
稅後淨利 1,114.5 億元，YoY+2713.11%，EPS 為 24.04 元。 
展望 2027 年，預期全球市場將面臨更為緊俏且吃緊的供需格局，而受惠於約 5K 的
產能擴充，Flash 2027 年的位元出貨量成長率可望突破 40%。此外，由於原廠陸續將
低容量 2D NAND EOL，促使低容量 eMMC 需求轉向 SLC NAND，使 SLC NAND 的
供給在 2027 年維持緊縮狀態。此外，在伺服器端，預估全新的AI 伺服器機櫃，單一
機櫃將採用達 500 顆至 600 顆的 NOR 與 NAND Flash 晶片，尤其拉動 512Mb、
1Gb 及 2Gb 等高容量快閃記憶體的出貨。DRAM 方面，預計 2027 年將增加來自於
Enterprise SSD 的營收貢獻，目前華邦電已經在進行送樣，看好 Enterprise SSD 需
求持續提升之下，可望為華邦電帶來額外 10%的 DRAM 營收貢獻。產能方面，如前
段所述，預計台中廠將額外擴增 5K Flash 產能，並且陸續降低 32nm 等舊製程投片
量，將產能轉移至較為先進的 Flash 製程；高雄廠則預計滿載月產能將達 24~25K，
並透過製程轉換，提高 16nm 製程比重，進一步推成位元出貨量。投研部預估 2027
年營收約 6,008 億元，YoY+114.64%，毛利率 71.21%，YoY+3.3ppts，稅後淨利為
2,550 億元，YoY+128.84%，EPS 為 54.03 元。 
展望後市，產品應用的層面將隨著 Edge AI、On-device AI 以及 Physical AI 的興起
而顯著擴張，尤其在邊緣端設備上，NOR Flash 及 NAND Flash 的容量與數量需求都
將出現爆發性成長；DRAM 則將隨著前述 eSSD 的需求增加而帶動出貨成長。為了因
應 2028 年後更為龐大的市場需求，華邦已經通過 731 億元的資本支出案，正式啟動
下一階段高雄廠 Module B 的擴產計畫。該擴建工程預計 2029 年 1 月開始裝機，預
計於 2H29 小量出貨。Module B 的無塵室面積約為目前高雄廠的兩倍，滿載產能可
望達 50K 至 60K 的月產能，預計將採分階段裝機投產。製程方面，Module B 將採用
16 奈米、14 奈米及 12 奈米製程，並規劃在第二階段引進 EUV。 
此外，針對矽電容佈局方面，公司目前具備 5,400 nF/mm²超高容值規格的技術，具
備極薄化、耐高溫與優異的高頻反應特性。面對 AI 晶片先進封裝空間緊湊及傳統
MLCC 缺貨之痛點，華邦電矽電容可直接嵌入PCB 或 CoWoS 中介層，滿足光模組與
AI 加速晶片對高頻穩定供電的需求。公司採 Foundry 定位，目前產能已被 MLCC 及
AI 供應商包光，並已於高雄廠 Module B 規劃專屬線擴產，預估 2027 年後將成為
CUBE 之外的重要動能。 
CUBE 及矽電容陸續貢獻營收，中長期營運動能正向 
整體而言 ， 隨著華邦電4Gb DDR4/DDR3 出貨占比提升 ， 以及3Q26 陸續出貨的 8Gb 
DDR4 加入，可望改善產品組合，配合報價漲勢提升獲利表現；Flash 方面除了前述
受供給面影響而使報價呈上行趨勢外，預期華邦電將持續維持全球第一大 NOR Flash
供應商的領導地位，並受惠於長期伺服器與 AI 高容量應用的滲透率提升，同時隨車
用 、 消費性電子產品規格升級 ， 對於NOR Flash 或 SLC NAND 的需求亦見同步增長 ，
故公司於 2026 年再度追加 NOR 及 NAND 的產能擴充資本支出，顯見其對於後市正

### Page 4

- 4 -

個股報告 
向看待；最後，在中長期方面，CUBE 目前已進入與客戶合作開發階段，主要針對 AI 
ASIC 及邊緣運算需求，其優勢在於採用3D 堆疊技術，4 層堆疊的頻寬可對標 HBM，
但功耗僅為其 20%，極具競爭力，預計將於 2027 年開始顯著貢獻營收，同時公司新
增矽電容業務，預計將成為公司長期成長的關鍵動能。 
評價方面，考量公司 3Q26 ASP 漲勢優於先前預期，且 4Q26 Flash 產品仍具上漲空
間，再加上 2027 年位元出貨量可望迎來顯著成長，營收成長動能強勁之餘，中長期
亦有 CUBE 以及矽電容營收貢獻，預期營運至 2028 年可望維持高成長態勢，故投研
部維持強力買進建議，目標價 205 元(2027F PE 4X)。

評等分級：買進（預期未來六個月股價漲幅超過+30%）; 逢低買進（預期未來六個月股價漲幅介於+15%與+30%之間）; 區間操
作（預期未來六個月股價漲跌幅介於-15%與+15%之間）; 逢高賣出（預期未來六個月股價跌幅介於-15%與-30%之間）；賣出（預
期未來六個月股價跌幅超過-30%）。 
本刊所刊載之內容僅做為參考，惟已力求正確與完整，但因時間及市場客觀因素改變所造成產業、市場或個股之相關條件改變，投資人需自行考量

- 1 - 
個股報告

台光電(2383) 
Q3 財報創新高，26/27年獲利上修 
投資評等 目標價 
買進 $ 6460 
前次投資建議：2026.08.05 
「買進」 ，目標價: $ 6945

報告日期：2026.11.09 研 究 員：邱岳霖

摘 要 及 調 整 原 因 
➢ 高階材料放量、報價持續上揚、新產能開出，上修 2026 年 EPS至 132 元 
➢ 2027 年高階材料規格升級、產能持續擴增， EPS 280.8元，考量公司在產業具有主導地位
且未來幾年將持續高速增長，不過因利率走升與市場面臨短期逆風，故下修估值，兆豐投顧
降低目標價至 6,460元(23X 27(F)PER)，潛在上漲空間 69.1%，投資評等維持「買進」 。

基本資料 結論與建議 
2026/11/6 收盤價： 3820.00 ➢ 展望後市，AI Server、高速交換器與General server需
求強勁，公司持續擴產，預計 2026年底月產能增至約
615萬張。AI Server規格提升帶動高階材料佔比墊高，
M9已於26Q3開始出貨，Q4佔比將持續擴大；BT材料
亦已進入認證，年底有機會放量。研究部認為高階CCL
缺料格局延續，加上原物料成本上升，報價有望再上調
5-20%。考量產品組合轉佳、漲價效應及 Q3財報優於
預期，上修台光電 26Q4營收至 700.2億元， QoQ 
+15.0%/YoY +180.9%，毛利率39.0%，稅後淨利175.9
億元，EPS 49.1元；2026年稅後淨利473.0億元，YoY 
+222.9%，EPS 132.0元。

- 2 - 
個股報告 
資料來源：CMoney；兆豐投顧預估

營 運 分 析

台光電為全球銅箔基板龍頭廠  
台光電成立於 1992 年並於 1998 年市，總部位於桃園觀音，為全球最大無鹵
素銅箔基板供應商，AI Server 中 CCL 市占率超過 50%，同時是 HDI 材料
龍頭。主力產品為銅箔基板 (CCL)、黏合片 (PP)及多層壓合板 (Mass 
Lamination)，不過自 2020 年起切入 IC載板材料(BT/ABF)之 CCL以作為第
二成長曲線，目前正積極推進客戶認證與產能擴充。

圖一、台光電為全球 CCL與 HDI 材料龍頭廠商

資料來源：公司；兆豐投顧整理

生產基地包含台灣桃園觀音、新竹，中國江蘇昆山、廣東中山、湖北黃石，
以及馬來西亞檳城。銅箔基板月產能現況約 585 萬張，預計 2026 年底增至
約 615 萬張，2027 年底目標達 900萬張，且近期已與客戶敲定至 2030 年產
能至 1,525 萬張。26Q3 以基礎設施應用(AI 伺服器、Switch、低軌衛星等)
為主，占比約 80%。

個股報告

圖二、台光電全球布局與產能規劃

資料來源：公司；兆豐投顧整理

報價上漲紅利，M9新產品出貨，營收與毛利率雙創高，Q3 EPS 40.5 元 
台光電 (2383)公告 26Q3 財報：營收 608.85 億元， QoQ +28.8%/YoY 
+142.1%，再創歷史新高，係因報價上揚，同時 M9 開始出貨帶動所致，毛
利率 37.7%，季增 3.8ppt，年增 7.6ppt，大幅優於研究部預期之 35%，受惠
報價上漲完整反映，同時產品組合轉佳帶動，不過因持續投入新世代產品研
發帶動整體營業費用較上季增長逾 30%，稅後淨利 145 億元， QoQ 
+46.9%/YoY +265.7%，EPS 40.5元，遠優於預期之 33.5 元。

- 4 - 
個股報告 
高階材料放量、報價持續上揚、新產能開出，上修 2026年 EPS至 132元 
展望後市，隨著 AI Server、高速交換器與 General server 需求持續強勁，公
司持續擴產以滿足市場需求，月產能預計 2026 年底增至約 615 萬張，季增
約 5%，此外由於 AI Server 規格持續提升，高階材料佔比將逐步墊高，同時
BT材料已開始認證，預期年底有機會開始放量出貨；原物料方面，研究部認
為高階 CCL缺料格局將延續，同時反映原物料成本上升，預期報價有望再上
調 5-20%，此外更高階材料 M9 於 26Q3 開始出貨，預期 Q4 佔比將持續擴
大。

在整體產品組合轉佳與漲價效應帶動下 ，同時考量 Q3 財報遠優研究部預
期，故上修台光電26Q4營收至 700.2億元(原估593億元)，QoQ +15.0%/YoY 
+180.9%，毛利率 39%(原估 35.5%)，季增 1.3ppt，年增 10.4ppt，稅後淨
利 175.9 億元，QoQ +21.3%/YoY +370.7%，EPS 49.1 元(原估 37.2 元)；
同步上修 2026年稅後淨利 473億元 ，YoY +222.9%，EPS 132元(原估 113.2
元)。

2027 年高階材料規格升級、產能持續擴增， EPS 280.8 元 
展望 2027 年，隨著 GPU 與 Switch 規格持續提升，帶動 PCB/CCL 層數與
尺寸持續提升，且 CSP持續擴大 Capex，ASIC需求亦繼續上調，目前公司
訂單能見度已達 2027 年，亦積極擴張產能，預期至 27 年底達月產能 900
萬張目標，年增近 50%。而客戶主動要求簽 LTA 並確保後續產能無虞，目
前台光電已通過至 2030 年產能建置計畫，預期將總產能大幅增加至 1,525
萬張，為現有產能的 2.5 倍，將有助於公司未來持續高速增長；此外 BT 載
板材料預計 26年底完成認證，27年開始出貨，初步規劃出貨量約 10 萬張，
不過為確保未來高速成長的需求，將規劃 120 萬張產能，預期將帶來新一波
成長動能。綜上所述，考量整體市場供需格局持續偏緊，且公司產能擴充積
極，輔以報價持續上升，兆豐投顧上修台光電 2027 年營收至 3697.2 億元(原
估 3,209.5 億元)，YoY +75.0%，而毛利率方面，預期在新一代 GPU 推出，
同時 Switch 亦升級至 3.2T 下，M9 材料營收佔比可望由今年低個位數提升
至雙位數，在稼動率維持高檔、產品組合持續優化下，上修毛利率至 41.2%(原
估 36.6%)，年增 5.2ppt，稅後淨利 1006.2億元，YoY +112.7%，EPS 280.8
元(原估 210 元)。

Greater China Technology Hardware
Asia Pacific
Industry View In-Line
(-) PC OEMs and ODMs: Intel (covered by Joseph Moore) expects 2H26 PC units to
fall 20%+ y/y as OEMs aggressively cut inventories amid weaker sell-through; the
correction has accelerated over the past two months. This aligns with Morgan

Stanley's estimate at a ~25% y/y decline  (report link).
(+) Server OEMs and ODMs: Intel guides for server CPU unit growth of 30%+ in
both 2026 and 2027, with upside if wafer/substrate supply improves. It sees server
CPU TAM rising from US$40bn today to >US$200bn in five years, driven mainly by
AI agents.
(+) ABF substrates:  Most of the previously discussed ~40% capacity increase is due
in 2H27; Intel expects key component purchase commitments to rise sharply over

the next several quarters. Intel is also prioritizing securing substrate capacity and
improving yields for the EMIB-T. Complex designs add yield risk, which could lower
effective output, worsening ABF substrate shortages but adding further support for
pricing.

Key Takeaways
We view negative PC comments as more bearish for OEMs and ODMs positioned
in the lower end consumer segment, like Acer and Compal.
Strong CPU unit growth would be positive for Wiwynn, Lenovo, and Lotes.
Tighter ABF substrate supply and stronger pricing would be positive for
Unimicron and NYPCB's revenue and margins.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.

below.

## Document 195: 261107_ml_wiwynn

Source PDF: all report pdf/261107_ml_wiwynn.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-11-07
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Wiwynn

AI-driven sales growth since 4Q; reiterate 
Buy and trim PO to NT$2,310

Reiterate Rating: BUY | PO: 2,310 TWD | Price: 1,555 TWD

3Q beat driven by both general and ASIC servers 
Wiwynn’s 3Q sales grew 56% QoQ/63% YoY, supported by sustained general server 
demand as well as a strong ramp of ASIC servers (e.g., AWS Trainium 3) from Sep. 
GM/OPM of 8.87%/7.47% was above BofAe of 7.77%/6.42% and consensus of 
8.42%/6.85%. OP was 40%/16% above BofAe/consensus. After a non-op. loss (mainly 
from higher interest expenses), NP was 34%/10% above BofAe/consensus.  
4Q sales led by ASIC; GPU sales expected by year-end 
The company posted strong Oct sales of NT$165.1bn (-4% MoM/+81% YoY), tracking 
35%/36% of BofA/consensus estimates. Its general server business was impacted by 
component shortages in Oct, despite demand remaining strong. For AI servers, we 
expect ASIC to be the major sales driver in 4Q with a strong ramp since Sep; while GPU 
servers (from multiple customers) are expected to deliver sales contribution by year-end.  
Solid sales growth in 2027-28; GM pressured by AI  
Our checks indicate that Wiwynn’s ASIC projects remain on a buy-and-sell model, 
although a consignment business model is under discussion; while business models for 
GPU projects have yet to be confirmed. With strong AI server momentum, we expect its 
GM to decline QoQ to 7.67% in 4Q26. For 2027/28, we model solid YoY sales growth of 
+60%/+31% , with conservative GMs of 6.67%/6.51% in 2027/28, vs. 8.30% in 2026E.  
Reiterate Buy; trim PO to NT$2,310 
We raise our 2026-28E EPS by 4-14%, mainly to reflect the 3Q26 beat and solid sales 
growth aided by general and ASIC server projects. We however trim PO to NT$2,310 
(15x 4Q26-3Q27E EPS) from NT$2,387 (18x 2H26-1H27E EPS), as we roll over our 
valuation base and lower multiple on macro uncertainties and concerns over margin 
decline. Reit. Buy on robust ASIC momentum and a constructive general server outlook.

Company Sector 
IT Hardware

Company Description 
Wiwynn supplies server, storage and cloud equipment to 
global cloud service providers (CSPs). 100% of its sales 
comes from server/datacenter business. Key clients are 
Meta, Microsoft, and Amazon. Wiwynn was founded in April 
2012 and was listed on Taiwan Stock Exchange on 27 
March 2019. As of 2Q26, Wistron (3231 TT) owned 35.27% 
of shares in Wiwynn. Besides the headquarter in Taiwan, its 
subsidiaries and operations are located in Malaysia, Hong 
Kong, China, South Korea, Japan, the US and Mexico.

Investment Rationale 
We have a Buy rating on Wiwynn as we are positive on 
increasing contribution from AI server projects, delivery in 
Amazon after efforts for years, and regular server 
bottoming out from 2H23, driving double-digit 
sales/earnings growth in 2026-28E.

### Page 5

Price objective basis & risk 
Wiwynn (XWIQF) 
Our PO of NT$2,310 is based on 15x 4Q26-3Q27E EPS, which is 1.5-2.0SD above 
Wiwynn's historical P/E average since listing. We believe such a multiple is reasonable to 
reflect Wiwynn's rising sales contribution from AI server, delivery in Amazon projects, 
and regular server demand bottoming out from 2H23.

Upside risks to our PO are: (1) higher-than-expected capex growth from key clients, (2) 
stronger-than-expected momentum in server/datacenter infrastructure, (3) more project 
wins/launches for AI server, and (4) much higher contribution from the third largest 
customer.

Downside risks to our PO are: (1) unexpected slowdown of key clients' capex spending in 
server/datacenter, (2) more intensified competition from peers, and (3) weaker 
momentum in AI server projects.

FUNDAMENTAL EQUITY OPINION KEY: Opinions include a Volatility Risk Rating, an Investment Rating and an Income Rating. VOLATILITY RISK RATINGS, indicators 
of potential price fluctuation, are: A - Low, B - Medium and C - High. INVESTMENT RATINGS reflect the analyst’s assessment of both a stock’s absolute total return 
potential as well as its attractiveness for investment relative to other stocks within its Coverage Cluster (defined below). Our investment ratings are: 1 -  Buy stocks 
are expected to have a total return of at least 10% and are the most attractive stocks in the coverage cluster; 2 - Neutral stocks are expected to remain flat or 
increase in value and are less attractive than Buy rated stocks and 3 - Underperform stocks are the least attractive stocks in a coverage cluster. An investment 
rating of 6 (No Rating) indicates that a stock is no longer trading on the basis of fundamentals. Analysts assign investment ratings considering, among other things, 
the 0-12 month total return expectation for a stock and the firm’s guidelines for ratings dispersions (shown in the table below). The current price objective for a 
stock should be referenced to better understand the total return expectation at any given time. The price objective reflects the analyst’s view of the potential price 
appreciation (depreciation). 
Investment rating Total return expectation (within 12-month period of date of initial 
rating) 
Ratings dispersion guidelines for coverage clusterR2 
Buy ≥ 10% ≤ 70% 
Neutral ≥ 0% ≤ 30% 
Underperform N/A ≥ 20% 
R2Ratings dispersions may vary from time to time where BofA Global Research believes it better reflects the investment prospects of stocks in a Coverage Cluster. 
INCOME RATINGS, indicators of potential cash dividends, are: 7 - same/higher (dividend considered to be secure), 8 - same/lower (dividend not considered to be 
secure) and 9 - pays no cash dividend.  Coverage Cluster is comprised of stocks covered by a single analyst or two or more analysts sharing a common industry, 
sector, region or other classification(s). A stock’ s coverage cluster is included in the most recent BofA Global Research report referencing the stock.

Simplo Technology (6121.TWO)
An Established Battery Franchise with Room for a Better Mix
CITI'S TAKE 
3Q26 EPS missed our/BBG estimates by 35%/30% each as notebook pull-
ins faded and low yields on a major US customer’s new smartphone held 
back shipments. We expect higher PC component costs to keep IT demand 
subdued in 1Q27E, while rising contribution from AES could still help margin 
expansion in 2027E. We lower 2026-2028E EPS by 17-20% and revise our 
TP to NT$400 based on 12x 2027E EPS, lowered from 13x reflecting weaker 
visibility on IT recovery. We continue to favor Simplo’s leading market share 
in notebook/smartphone battery franchises and its exposure to AIDC BBU 
growth through AES. 
3Q earnings -17% y/y missed our/BBG estimates by 35%/30% on weaker IT 
shipments — 3Q26 sales of NT$19.9bn fell 2% y/y and 5% q/q, 13%/10% below 
our/BBG forecasts. Notebook customers pulled orders into 1H26 amid rising 
memory prices, while lower yields on a major US customer’s new smartphone 
constrained 3Q shipment. Group GPM rose 0.7ppt y/y to 16.3% on a larger AES 
contribution. However, standalone GPM dropped 1.6ppt y/y 50 9.2% on higher input 
costs and lower volumes. Group OPM rose 0.4ppt y/y to 9.7%, lower than our (12.2%) 
and BBG (11.9%) estimates. Higher taxes also weighed on EPS. 
2027E outlook stays muted for IT — The mgmt. expects IT demand to remain 
lukewarm in 1Q27E as higher component costs put upward pressure on PC prices 
likely weighing on demand. Standalone sales are expected to be flat to slightly down 
y/y in 2027E, with the group sales flat to slightly up on AES’s BBU growth. Mgmt. 
notes the battery-pack inventories at notebook brands are lean at just a few weeks 
and easing cobalt prices could bring down ASP in 2027E. A new non-IT product line 
is growing severalfold from a small base, and the mgmt. expects it to make up >5% 
in 2028E with a GPM above group’s average. 
Implications — We cut 2026-2028E earnings estimates 17-20% for 2026-2028E 
reflecting 3Q miss and a slower IT recovery, resulting in a 10-18% cut on our sales 
forecasts. We lower our TP to NT$400 based on a target P/E of 12x from 13x on 2027E 
EPS, reflecting the component inflation likely delaying the timing of IT recovery. We 
stay Buy on Simplo’s established notebook and smartphone battery franchises and 
AES’s exposure to growing datacenter BBU demand. AI PC adoption could lift 
battery-pack ASPs over time, and an estimated 6% dividend yield should help 
cushion downside. 
n
Angela Hsu AC

Simplo Technology (6121.TWO)
09 November 2026 Citi Research

Simplo Technology
Company description
Established in 1992, Simplo specializes in designing and producing battery
packs for Notebooks, tablet PC, and electronic vehicle products. With strong
execution and cost advantages, Simplo continues to broaden its client base.
Its major customers include PC OEMs (such as HPQ, Dell, Asus, Acer, and
Apple) and NB ODMs (such as Quanta, Compal, Wistron, and Inventec).

Investment strategy
We rate Simplo shares as Buy. Simplo’s heating fundamentals are driven by
the following reasons. 1) Japan supplier's strategy change on focusing EV
battery pack and de-prioritizing NB which provides Simplo more orders wins;
and 2)  Non-IT: according to our industry survey, Simplo has been qualified by
global ISPs for the battery back units (BBU) for their data centers.
Valuation
Our target price of NT$400 is based on a target P/E of 12x 2027E EPS, on par

with its historical average since 2010.
Risks
Downside risks that may cause the shares to continue to trade below our
target price include: 1) weaker than expected end product sales; 2) intensified
competition; and 3) worse-than-expected overall NB/tablet/PC growth.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

our ratings distribution table for our Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside rating system. However, we reiterate that we do
not consider No View to be a recommendation. For all Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside calls, risk exists that the
catalyst(s) and associated share-price movement will not materialize as expected.

營收走勢圖

研究員/游周翰 Jeffery.Yu@uni-psg.com

研究部維持聯亞買進投資建議，主要考量：(1)3Q26 營收及獲利皆
優於市場預期；(2)AI Data Center 矽光需求持續強勁，主要客戶
訂單能見度超過 6 個月，4Q26 營收可望續增，1Q27 新增產能開出
將帶動營收明顯跳增；(3)管理層維持先前 2027 年產能擴增指引
(+150%YoY)，並且確立2028 年產能擴增速度與 2027 年相當，亦即
兩年擴增產能超過 6 倍。惟考量(1)2026 年新增後段產能驗證時間
較研究部預期長；(2)3Q26 毛利率及費用率優於預期；(3)2027 年
產能增長幅度優於研究部預期 ， 因此調整2026/2027 年營收分別為
55.2 億元(原估 55.92 億)及 155.6 億元(原估 150.4 億)，EPS 分別
為 20.95 元(原估 19.96 元)及 58.76 元(原估 54.06 元)。目標從
3,800 上修至 4,000 元，重申買進投資建議。

重點摘要： 
1. 3Q26營收、EPS均優於市場預期，矽光出貨帶動獲利成長： 
聯亞3Q26營收15.62億元(+27.9%QoQ，+182.6%YoY)，略低研究
部預期，主因為後段產能驗證時間較研究部預期長 ； 營收雙增
主要還是受惠AI Data Center 矽光產品出貨量持續增加。毛
利率58.6%(+1.1pts QoQ，+15 pts YoY)，營業利益為7.71億
元，營益率達49.4%；營業費用1.43億元(+11%QoQ，+4%YoY)，
主因為研發及員工分紅費用增加；稅後淨利 6.21億元，EPS 
6.12元， 優於研究部預期 ， 主因為毛利率及費用控管優於研究
部預估。產品組合方面，Data Center占營收80–85%，其中以
矽光產品為主 ；Telecom占5–10%， 其他約5%， 與上季比重相當。

表一、3Q26 研究部預估與實際差異 
單位：百萬元 實際 研究部原估 差異 
營業收入 1,562 1,596 -2.1% 
營業利益 771 713 +8.1% 
稅後淨利 621 575 +8.0% 
EPS(元) 6.12 5.65 +8.0% 
資料來源： 聯亞財報、統一研究部預估

### Page 2

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  2

2. 4Q26營收持續季增，1Q27新增產能帶動出貨加速： 
展望4Q26，主要客戶目前訂單期間已超過6個月，管理層表示
在手訂單金額已超過2026年全年營收規模， 顯現出目前需求
仍 然 十 分 強 勁 。 管 理 層 預 期 營 收 季 增幅 約 與4Q25 相當
(+16.2%QoQ)，即18.15億元 。 然而 ， 依照過往管理層給予的指
引通常較為保守，且在需求強勁以及新加入的 合作夥伴陸續
開出 後 段 產 能 下 ， 研 究 部 預 估 4Q26 營收為 18.32 億元
(+17.3%QoQ，+185.2%YoY)，EPS為6.77元。

展望1Q27，隨相關設備導入及後段委外產能開始量產，管理層
預期1Q27產出量將有更明顯增幅 。研究部預估 1Q27營收為
275.2億元(+50.2%QoQ，+204%YoY)，EPS為10.26。

3. 美國客戶四年約加持，2028年產出目標為2026年的6.25倍： 
聯亞8M26與美系Data Center 客戶簽訂2027–2030年為期四年
的CW Laser供貨長約 ， 客戶並以保證金方式提供資金支持 ， 以
確保未來產品供應 。 為履行長期供貨承諾 ， 公司同步追加近30
億元資本支出計畫，使過去一年累計核准資本支出超過 80億
元 ， 預計2027年產出顆數較2026年增加150%，2028年再較2027
年增加150%， 相當於2028年產出規模達2026年的6.25倍 ， 其中
8月新增資本支出主要貢獻於2028年。此外，為確保擴產後的
原料供應，公司亦與兩家日系及一家美系InP基板供應商簽訂
長期採購合約 ， 並持續增加台灣及海外後段委外代工夥伴 。 研
究部認為，美系客戶透過長期供貨合約及保證金支持聯亞擴
產 ， 顯示客戶對未來CW Laser需求具有高度確定性 ， 亦有助提
高新增產能的去化能見度，支撐公司2027–2028年營運成長。

4. 多波長CW Laser具技術及ASP優勢，NPO較CPO先行： 
聯亞800G及1.6T矽光產品已進入量產，分別具備8顆雷射及8
至16顆不同波長雷射的解決方案。管理層指出，多波長產品
ASP較單波長高出50%以上，且全球具備大規模量產能力的供
應商有限 ， 有利公司維持產品競爭優勢 。 隨未來光通訊傳輸規
格持續升級，公司亦積極開發3.2T及NPO/CPO應用所需的高功
率CW Laser。其中，管理層預期NPO有望於2H27率先作為過渡
方案，目前所接觸客戶以外部光源設計為主；CPO則因光源配
置 、 連接器等相關規格尚未完全定案 ， 大規模導入時程仍有待
觀察。

### Page 3

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  3

此外，隨NPO/CPO等應用對光源功率要求提高，未來單顆 CW 
Laser功率提升至250–300mW以上 。 管理層指出 ， 高功率產品因
晶粒面積增加及良率下降，相同InP基板產能可產出的晶粒顆
數可能減少30–50%，使高功率光源對基板及有效產能的需求
增加 。 研究部認為 ， 短期聯亞仍以既有CW Laser產品擴產及多
波長方案出貨成長為主要動能，中長期則隨 NPO/CPO逐步導
入 ， 高功率光源有望成為新增需求來源 ， 並提高相關產品的製
造及供應門檻，將有望成為其技術護城河，維持競爭優勢。

5. Data Center高速PD完成驗證，預計隨新增產能逐步量產： 
除CW Laser外，聯亞亦持續布局Data Center高速PD產品。管
理層指出 ，200G以上PD已完成客戶驗證 ， 預計2027年新增產能
陸續開出，開始量產 Data Center PD 產品。惟目前仍以 CW 
Laser為主要生產重心，因此PD產出規模相對有限，但有助於
降低產品集中度風險。

評等  定義  
強力買進     預估未來 6 個月內的絕對報酬超過 25%以上

주가 추이

3Q26 매 출액 과 영 업 이익 은 비현 금 성 비용 항목 개 선 으로 시장 기대치
를 상 회 했 으나, 차량 판매 감소와 인 플 레 이션 등으로 연간 가이 던 스 는 
하 향 되 었 습니 다. 올해 생산 차질 에 따라 이 연된 신차 판 매를 ’27년에 
확대하며 매출/이익 회복을 가시 화 하 는 부분 에 주목 필 요 합니다. 
매출 성장은 금융부문이, 영업이익은 자동차부문이 기여 
동사 3Q26 매출액은 45.1조원(-3.5% 이하 Y oY), 영업이익 2.68조원
(+5.5%)을 기록하며 시장 기대치를 상회했다. 파업으로 인한 생산 차질
로 3Q 판매량 92.1만대(-11.3%)에 그치며 자동차부문 매출은 큰 폭으
로 감소(33조원, -10.0%)했으나, 금융부문 매출이 취급 자산 증가로 큰 
폭으로 성장(+27.8%)했다. 반면, 영업이익은 금리 상승에 따른 금융부문 
영업비용 확대 영향과 기타부문 실적 부진 발생과 달리 자동차부문은 전
년 동기 대비 관세율 차이에 따른 비용 감소와 판매보증 충당부채 전입
액 감소 등 우호적 환율효과로 큰 폭의 증가세(+24.8%) 기록했다. 
’26년 가이던스 하향, 구조적 반등 요인은 아직 
3Q 실적 발표에서 주목할 부분은 가이던스 하향과 현금흐름이다. 연간 
판매목표를 연초 415.8만대에서 400만대로 하향했고 영업이익률 목표
는 6.3~7.3%에서 5.5~6.5%로 0.8%p 하향했으며, ’26년 투자계획은 
17.8조원에서 16조원으로 낮췄다. 특히, 투자계획 하향은 차량 판매 매
출 감소 대비 인센티브, 원자재 가격 상승 등으로 현금흐름이 악화된 
부분에 기인한 것으로 판단된다. 실적발표에서 전사적 원가 최적화 활
동 추진을 강조했으나, 이는 좀 더 장기적 측면에서 효과가 가시화될 
것으로 판단되며, 인플레이션 및 원화절상 상황 고려 시 구조적 반등은 
단기적으로 쉽지 않을 수 있다. 
절치부심의 2027년, 목표주가 600.000원 유지 
그러나 매크로 상황을 제 외 하 면  ’26년 하반기 생산 차질에 따른 신차 
판매효과가 ’27년 으 로 이연된 것 이 라 는 점에 주목 필요하다.   ‘북미 HEV, 유럽 
BEV’ 전략 하에서 엘란트라, 투싼 풀세인지, GV80, G80 등 고수익 HEV 차종 
중심 미국 수요 대응을 통해 Mix 개선하는 모습이 확인되고, 생산이 본 격 화 된 
아이오닉3 유럽 판매 확대 시 중국 로 컬 업 체 들 의 공세를 방 어 하 는 모 습 을 
확인하는 것이 가 능 할 것으로 판 단 한 다. 노 후 차 종 중심 운 영 에 따른 판매 둔화 
이외에 아직 근 원 적 판매 경쟁력 저하로는 판단하지 않는다.

저희 회사는 공표일 현재 이 자료에서 다룬 종목의 발행주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다.

합니다. 이 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료나 정보출처로부터 얻은 것이지만, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완
전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 이 자료는 어떠한 경우에도 고객의 증권투자 결과와 관련된 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙으로 사
용될 수 없습니다.

### Page 6

현대차 (005380) [한화리서치]

6 
[ 종목 투자등급 ] 
당사는 개별 종목에 대해 향후 1 년간 +15% 이상의 절대수익률이 기대되는 종목에 대해 Buy(매수) 의견을 제시합니다. 또한 절대수
익률 -15~+15%가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Hold(보유) 의견을, -15% 이하가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Sell(매도) 의견을 제시합니다. 밸류
에이션 방법 등 절대수익률 산정은 개별 종목을 커버하는 애널리스트의 추정에 따르며, 목표주가 산정이나 투자의견 변경 주기는 종
목별로 다릅니다.

[ 산업 투자의견 ] 
당사는 산업에 대해 향후 1 년간 해당 업종의 수익률이 과거 수익률에 비해 양호한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Positive(긍정
적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 또한 향후 1 년간 수익률이 과거 수익률과 유사한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Neutral(중립적) 
의견을, 과거 수익률보다 부진한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Negative(부정적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 산업별 수익률 전
망은 해당 산업 내 분석대상 종목들에 대한 담당 애널리스트의 분석과 판단에 따릅니다.

[ 당사 조사분석자료의 투자등급 부여 비중 ] (기준일: 2026 년 9 월 30 일)

## Document 274: [005930] 삼성전자(005930) 결국 제자리를 찾아갈 것삼성전자(005930) 결국 제자리를 찾아갈 것결국 제자리를 찾아갈 것 - 기업

 결국 제자리를 찾아갈 것

투자의견 BUY  
매수, 유지 
6개월 목표주가 500,000 
유지  
현재주가 
(26.10.29) 
231,000 
반도체업종

◼ 3분기 실적발표에 대한 시장의 반응은 다소 냉소적. 
◼ 주주환원은 시점의 문제일 뿐. 강화 방향성은 여전히 유효. 
◼ 메모리반도체 이익의 폭증이 가격의 매력을 유발. 비중확대 전략 유효.

매력적인 가격, 변하지 않은 주주환원 정책의 방향성 
목표주가와 매수의견 유지. 메모리반도체 주도 폭발적 이익 상승이 가격 (주
가)의 매력을 지속 유발하고 있고, 주주환원 정책의 강화 방향성에도 변한 것
은 없다는 판단. 절대적으로 싼 주가를 비중 확대의 기회로 삼을 필요. 
1. 주주환원, 시점의 문제 
금번 실적발표에 대한 시장의 반응은 다소 냉소적. 대외 불확실성이 큰 만큼, 
시장은 자사주 매입/소각과 같은 즉각적 주가 부양 정책의 발표를 기대.  
당사는 삼성전자의 주주환원이 “시점의 문제”라 생각. 총 주주환원의 규모도, 
차기 (2027-2029년) 주주환원 정책의 강화 방향에도 변한 것은 없다는 판
단. 시장이 기대하는 자사주 매입/소각은 우선주 중심으로 시행될 가능성이 
높으며, 시행 시, 그 규모는 25조원 내외일 것 (DPS 12,567원)으로 전망. 
2. 메모리반도체, 더 올라갈 것 
보수적 환율 전망 (2027년 평균 환율 1,300원 적용)에도, 2027년 메모리반도
체 영업이익은 608조원에 달할 것. 이익 전망의 상향 여력 또한 유효. 
1) 최대 생산, 최대 판매 
삼성전자는 금일 4Q26 DRAM 판매량 가이던스로 전분기 대비 10% 중후반 
성장 (q-q +18%로 추정)을 제시. 일부 투자자들은 높아진 기저 부담으로 
2027년 판매 성장이 제한될 것을 우려. 당사는 높아진 기저에도 2027년 
DRAM 판매가 전년대비 20% 상승할 것으로 전망. 
- 극단적 수급 불균형 속, 삼성전자는 “최대 생산, 최대 판매” 정책을 시행 
중. 삼성전자만의 강력한 Execution이 20% 대 성장을 유지시킬 원동력. 
- DRAM 1c 수율의 조기 개선 속, 범용 DRAM 양산에 DRAM 1c Capa가 일
부 투입될 것으로 예상. Bit 성장을 높일 수 있는 변화가 될 것. 
2) 열려 있는 가격의 상방 
1Q27 모바일 DRAM에서 Surprise가 발생할 것이라는 의견을 유지 (전분기 
대비 30% 이상 상승). 장기계약이 체결된 CSP향으로도 가격의 추가 상승 여
력이 존재한다는 점을 상기할 필요. CSP의 경우, 당장의 비용 부담보다 AI 시
장 선점을 우선시. 장기계약으로 형성된 가격 Band의 천장 (Ceiling) 또한 시
장 우려 대비 낮지 않음을 감안할 필요. 2027년 범용 DRAM ASP는 전년대비 
40% 중후반까지 상승할 가능성 (현 전망: 전년대비 34% 상승).  
3) HBM, 제품 경쟁력과 점유율의 동반 성장  
최근 삼성전자향으로 HBM4/HBM4e 추가 공급 요청이 쇄도하고 있는 것으
로 추정. 일부 고객은 연내 추가 공급 시 2027년 계약 가격을 선제 지급할 
의사를 표출. 제품 경쟁력이 회복되었음을 보여주는 증거라 생각. 
제품 경쟁력의 회복과 최대 Capa의 이점 속, 2027년은 HBM 점유율 1위를 
확보하는 기념비적 해가 될 것으로 전망 (매출액 기준 점유율 38% 예상).

## Document 312: AI in 2027_ 20 questions for the global economy

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI in 2027_ 20 questions for the global economy.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-05
Pages: 24
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The AI boom is reshaping growth, productivity, trade, jobs and
markets. Investment still has room to run in 2027, but
ﬁnancing is getting riskier and expectations are sky-high. We
ask how long it can last, who stands to gain and what happens
if the promised returns fail to arrive
Carsten Brzeski, Bert Colijn, James Knightley, Deepali Bhargava,
James Smith, Lynn Song, Jan Frederik Slijkerman and Amrita Naik
Nimbalkar
In a nutshell
The AI boom still has room to run. Investment is likely to

continue rising in 2027, supported by strong demand
and constrained supply. But its contribution to US growth
is probably close to its peak as the sheer scale of
investment makes further acceleration harder.
The boom is entering a new phase. The story is
expanding beyond chips and data centres into power
generation, grids and cooling. At the same time,
ﬁnancing is shifting gradually from hyperscalers'
enormous cash ﬂows towards debt, private credit and

### Page 2

more complex structures, increasing the risks if expected
returns fail to materialise.
The economic eﬀects are becoming broader and more
complicated. AI is supporting US growth and global
technology supply chains, but may also be diverting
investment from other parts of the economy. It is adding
to inﬂationary pressures today through demand for
energy, equipment and skilled labour, while the hoped-
for productivity and disinﬂation beneﬁts are likely to take

longer to emerge.
The winners will not necessarily be those building the
most AI infrastructure. Europe and Asia are beneﬁting
from critical positions in the supply chain, while
ultimately the largest gains may go to companies and
economies that use AI most eﬀectively.
Our base case is a plateau, not a bust. Today's leading
technology companies are highly proﬁtable and much
less leveraged than their dotcom predecessors. But
expectations are exceptionally high. The crucial question

for 2027 is whether AI revenues and productivity gains
begin to justify the extraordinary investment. If they do
not, overcapacity, tighter ﬁnancing and falling valuations
could turn a plateau into a much harder landing.
How big, how long?
Railroads, electriﬁcation, dotcom ﬁbre: how
does today's AI boom compare with the
great investment manias of the past?
Bigger than the dotcom boom, more important for growth and, so
far, more safely ﬁnanced.
Investment in US data centre structures, computers and software

reached 5.6% of GDP in the second quarter of 2026, up from 3.8% in
2022, or 5.2% once imported hardware is netted out. That already

### Page 3

puts the current boom above the dotcom peak in 2000, when
business investment in computers, communications equipment
and software reached about 4.5% of GDP, although still below
Britain’s railway mania of the 1840s, when railway investment
alone reached roughly 6-7% of GDP.
So while this is not the biggest investment boom in history, it is
already bigger than the last great technology boom. And it is
having a much bigger economic impact: IT investment accounted

for 16-18% of US GDP growth in 1998-2000; AI-related investment
now accounts for 37%.
The biggest diﬀerence though is how this boom is being ﬁnanced.
Railways and telecom networks were built on debt and ended in
defaults, largely through unrated railroad bonds in the 19th century
and high-yield telecom debt after 2000. This time, investment has
so far been funded mainly from the cash ﬂows of some of the
world’s most proﬁtable companies, with debt and equity only now

being added at the margin.
The assets are diﬀerent, too. Rails and ﬁbre lasted for decades. Data
centre shells, grids and power plants will also have long lives, but
GPUs may have an economic life of just three to six years. That
much shorter shelf life is central to the debate over whether today’s
investment will generate suﬃcient returns.
Still, the payoﬀ could come faster. Factories took decades to
redesign their ﬂoors around the electric motor. AI coding tools are

paying oﬀ within months, and adoption is already disrupting
software, accounting, online retail and advertising.
The common thread is that transformative technology does not
necessarily mean good returns for those who ﬁnance the
transformation. The Nasdaq lost almost 80% between 2000 and
2002, yet the ﬁbre laid during the boom went on to underpin the
streaming economy. Investors build the road; others get to drive on
it.
How much more will be invested in AI in

2027, and is the boom still accelerating?

### Page 4

Still accelerating in dollars, but probably peaking in its
contribution to growth.
Momentum remains strong. Taiwan's leading semiconductor
companies report order books that are full on a rolling 18-month
basis, with any new capacity snapped up as soon as it comes
online. Industry forecasts point to tight supply-demand conditions
until 2028. Barring a wave of order cancellations or defaults, that
points to another year of heavy AI investment in 2027, with

hyperscaler capex expected to exceed US$1 trillion, increasingly
geared towards AI infrastructure.
So is the boom peaking? In dollar terms, no. The constraint today is
not appetite but supply: chips, power, permits and skilled
electricians. When the bottleneck is the factory gate rather than the
order book, spending is more likely to be stretched out than cut
back.
But economists should be careful what they wish for. GDP growth
responds to the change in investment, not its level. Investment that

rises 50% a year adds a lot to growth; investment that rises 10%
from a much bigger base adds far less; and investment that merely
holds steady at a record high adds nothing at all. As the base gets
bigger, the arithmetic gets tougher. That is why we expect AI
investment to keep rising in 2027 while its contribution to US
growth should peak.
What would signal a genuine top? Shortening lead times for chips
and equipment, cancelled or postponed data centre projects, capex

guidance that stops being revised up, and a widening gap between
investment and AI revenues.
For now, the bigger risk is not that the money runs out in 2027, but
that it keeps ﬂowing so fast that the questions about returns grow
much louder in 2028.
Chips and data centres are only the ﬁrst
layer. How big is the second wave of

### Page 5

investment in grids, power generation and
cooling?
Bigger, slower and longer-lived than the ﬁrst wave – and
potentially more important for the macro outlook.
As a rule of thumb, US$1bn buys roughly 25MW of fully equipped
AI compute capacity. Not all of the c.US$1 trillion of hyperscaler
capex expected in 2027 will go to AI compute; some will fund
conventional cloud infrastructure. But even if only half does, that
implies more than 12GW of additional power demand – roughly the

output of a dozen large nuclear reactors, needed around the clock.
That is where the second wave comes in: transmission lines,
substations and transformers, gas turbines and renewables,
battery storage and industrial cooling. In the US, grid connection
queues and transformer lead times have already become binding
constraints, and some developers are building their own generation
on site rather than waiting for the grid.
This second wave diﬀers from the ﬁrst in three ways. First, the

assets last for decades rather than years. A GPU may be obsolete
by 2030; a substation built today will still be humming in 2060.
That makes grid investment less vulnerable to a sudden AI
downturn and more like the railway build-out of the 19th century.
Second, it is slower. Permitting, planning and construction take
years, which spreads the investment impulse over time and could
stretch out the cycle. Third, it is more widely shared: grid and power

equipment draws on suppliers in Europe and Asia as well as US
construction and engineering ﬁrms, so the beneﬁts travel further.
There is a catch. Somebody has to pay for the grid, and in
regulated markets that somebody is often the household electricity
customer. Rising power bills are already turning data centres into a
political issue in parts of the US, and they feed straight into the
inﬂation debate.
Chips are the sprint. Power is the marathon – and the marathon

may end up mattering more.

### Page 6

Is this an American party, or does Europe
get an invitation? Who supplies the picks
and shovels?
So far it is very much an American party – but Europe supplies the
catering, and may turn out to be the smarter guest.
The investment gap is stark. US imports of computer equipment
and semiconductors, a rough proxy for AI investment, have risen by
about 258% since 2019, compared with 38% in the UK. Europe's
own build-out is held back by high energy prices, scarce grid

capacity – think of the data centre moratoria in Dublin and
Amsterdam – and fragmented capital markets. The EU's AI
gigafactories initiative is an attempt to catch up.
But Europe sells crucial picks and shovels. ASML expects €44-45bn
of sales in 2026 and says customers keep raising their equipment-
demand forecasts because of the scale and duration of AI
investment. Inﬁneon expects its AI-related revenues to rise from
around €1.5bn in FY2026 to €2.5bn in FY2027. Add electrical and

grid equipment, cables, power generation and cooling, and
Europe's industrial base is well plugged into the American build-
out.
Asia has been one of the biggest beneﬁciaries of the AI boom. The
gains extend well beyond Korea and China, which have beneﬁted
from surging demand for memory chips and advanced technology
hardware. Across ASEAN, economies have also attracted signiﬁcant
FDI into semiconductors, electronics manufacturing, and
supporting supply chains as ﬁrms expand capacity and diversify

production networks.
More importantly, the broad economic gains from AI come from
using it, not building it – and here Europe is surprisingly good.
Uptake is strong, and European SMEs use AI almost as intensively
as large corporates, because the upfront investment is low. Nor
does AI look like a winner-takes-all technology: switching costs are
low and network eﬀects limited. If model providers end up
competing on price, the spoils go to the users – and Europe does

### Page 7

not need to build nearly as many data centres to compete. Late to
the construction site, Europe could still be early to move in.
There is a silver lining, too. Europe is far less exposed if the boom
turns to bust. Its AI capex is smaller and the wealth channel much
weaker: equities make up 13% of UK households' ﬁnancial assets,
versus 44% in the US. Missing the party also means skipping the
hangover.
Which sectors stand to win most, and which
risk being left behind?

The winners sell the infrastructure or use AI to do more; the losers
are those whose business model is the very task AI now performs.
The clearest winners are the suppliers of the build-out:
semiconductors and chip equipment, utilities and grid operators,
electrical equipment, construction, cooling and data centre real
estate. They are already seeing it in their order books.
Software is mixed. AI is a powerful selling point, but it also
threatens the per-seat pricing model: if one employee with an AI

agent does the work of ﬁve, the customer needs fewer licences.
Most at risk are legacy IT services and business process
outsourcing, call centres, parts of professional services, and media
and advertising models that depend on search traﬃc. AI-based
services may also disrupt online retail, including online travel
agencies, as AI assistants increasingly do the browsing and the
booking.
But don't write oﬀ every exposed sector. The Jevons paradox –
cheaper supply raising demand – could favour some of them. Legal

services is a good example: if AI makes contract review or legal
advice much cheaper, demand may rise faster than the cost per
task falls. Growth may also come from unexpected places. In
healthcare, AI-driven drug discovery and diagnostics could open up
new treatments, creating demand rather than merely cutting costs.
Ultimately, productivity gains depend more on the tasks
employees perform than on the sector they sit in. Workers handling

### Page 8

large amounts of data, text or graphics are likely to see the largest
gains. The chart below, from our 2023 work, shows how the
potential beneﬁts of generative AI across tasks relate to the degree
of digitalisation across sectors: the more digital a sector already is,
the faster AI can travel through it.
In short, the winners will be those that build AI or use it to work
smarter. The losers will be those whose core activities can
increasingly be automated.

Growth, prices and trade
How much of US growth in 2027 will come
from AI capex, and what would growth look
like without it?
Today, more than a third. In 2027, probably less – which is exactly
why the rest of the economy matters.
At ﬁrst glance, AI looks like the engine of the entire US economy.
Data centre construction, equipment investment and software
spending have grown at a pace that makes their headline
contribution to GDP growth look spectacular. But much of that

money leaves the country straight away: from Taiwanese chips to
servers assembled abroad, the hardware is largely imported.
Netting out those imports, we estimate that AI-related investment
accounts for 37% of US real GDP growth. Substantial, but a long
way from the 'AI is the only game in town' narrative.
The more uncomfortable ﬁnding lies outside tech. Rather than
lifting the overall growth rate, the AI boom seems to have
cannibalised growth elsewhere. Strip out AI, and the rest of US

private non-residential investment had fallen year-on-year for six
consecutive quarters by mid-2026. Before 2021, tech and non-tech
investment moved broadly in step; since then, they have moved in
opposite directions. One explanation: shareholders reward
companies that embrace AI, so capital budgets are being redirected
towards tech at the expense of everything else. So the boom isn't

### Page 9

so much about creating additional investment as redirecting
investment from elsewhere in the economy.
What would US growth look like without AI? Not simply 37% lower.
Part of the capital, engineers and electricity absorbed by AI would
have found other uses. A world without the AI boom would most
likely show weaker growth, but not by the full amount of AI's
contribution – and with a more balanced investment mix.
For 2027, our plateau base case implies a smaller AI contribution to

growth. That would give the non-tech economy room to breathe –
but only if it is ready to take over.
For now, AI is a capex story, not a consumer story. And capex
stories have a habit of ending with a hangover.
The productivity question: when, if ever,
does AI show up in the statistics?
In the US and China, it already does – partly. The big gains are still
to come, and may arrive abruptly.
It depends on where you look. In the US, labour productivity has

picked up markedly since the start of the AI boom. Part of that
comes from high-skilled services, exactly where you would expect
AI to show up ﬁrst. But part of it reﬂects the enormous data centre
investments, which are capital-heavy and need very little labour.
Total factor productivity – the best available measure of gains from
technological progress – has improved decently in the US, but
without an exceptional acceleration. While AI-related sectors are

providing a signiﬁcant boost to several Asian economies, this
largely reﬂects rapid investment and higher capital utilisation
rather than a sustained improvement in underlying productivity.
However, in China, we’ve seen labour productivity growth pick up in
the last two years, breaking what had been an extended downtrend
as Chinese companies race to incorporate AI in workﬂows. In the
eurozone, which lacks comparable AI investment, labour
productivity growth remains lacklustre, with very few exceptions,

like for example, in the Netherlands.

### Page 10

The technology holds real promise for broad-based productivity
gains. But reaping them requires wider adoption and investment in
infrastructure and skills, both within companies and across the
economy. That suggests a J-curve: heavy upfront intangible
investment – time spent learning new skills and reorganising
workﬂows – could initially mute measured productivity before the
investment pays oﬀ, possibly quite abruptly. Electriﬁcation is the
classic precedent: the electric motor arrived in the 1880s, but

factory productivity only took oﬀ in the 1920s, once factories had
been rebuilt around it.
And even then, a shift of the workforce towards sectors where
productivity gains are harder to achieve – think healthcare and care
work – would dampen the macroeconomic eﬀect. Measurement
doesn't help either: when AI makes a free digital service better, GDP
statistics barely notice.
Still, after decades of slowing productivity growth, AI is likely to be a
game changer. The question is not so much whether it will show up

in the statistics, but when. In 1987, Robert Solow quipped that you
could see the computer age everywhere but in the productivity
statistics. It took about a decade to prove him wrong.
Is AI inﬂationary or deﬂationary?
Electricity, memory chips and construction
costs point one way; long-run efﬁciency
gains point the other.
Inﬂationary today, disinﬂationary tomorrow – but don't hold your
breath for the outright deﬂation Silicon Valley promises.
It depends on your time horizon. For now, the eﬀect runs in the

inﬂationary direction. AI's enormous energy demand is adding to
the current energy shock, memory chip prices have surged, and the
scramble for electricians, transformers and construction capacity is
pushing up costs. Meanwhile, the investment boom is pouring fuel
on the ﬁre of a US economy already running with above-target
inﬂation.

### Page 11

In the longer run, once the technology lifts productivity, it should
lower unit labour costs and exert disinﬂationary pressure across the
economy. Like most technological breakthroughs, AI will probably
end up being disinﬂationary. But the eﬀect will be concentrated in
services and much smaller for goods with physical inputs.
Algorithms can't make steel, copper or concrete cheaper.
There is also a less comfortable twist. AI allows companies to price

dynamically, adjusting prices to what each customer is willing to
pay and thereby capturing more of the consumer surplus. That
could make some prices stickier rather than lower. Together with
the physical constraints, this makes the outright deﬂation Sam
Altman envisages rather unlikely.
For AI itself, the picture is diﬀerent. As we argued in 2024, we expect
a downward price trend for AI services, as with other software
categories, and at the sector level we continue to hold that view.

Competition between model providers should keep the pressure on.
For central banks, this means the AI disinﬂation dividend is a story
for the second half of the decade, not for 2027. Until then, the
boom adds to demand faster than it adds to supply – the textbook
recipe for inﬂation, not deﬂation.
How is AI reshaping global trade and
investment ﬂows, from Taiwanese chips to
Dutch lithography?
AI is redrawing global trade around a single supply chain – and

rewarding the countries that own its choke points.
AI is increasingly sorting the global economy into winners and
laggards. The IMF has raised its outlook for economies beneﬁting
from the AI investment boom, particularly those embedded in the
global technology supply chain, while countries outside it see
much less of a boost. Taiwan is riding booming semiconductor
demand, South Korea is beneﬁting from demand for high-
bandwidth memory, and the eﬀects travel upstream to the

suppliers of advanced chipmaking equipment, including the
Netherlands.

### Page 12

This illustrates how AI is reshaping both trade and investment
ﬂows. Spending on data centres and computing capacity in the US
drives demand for chips from Asia, which in turn requires
investment in semiconductor capacity and equipment from Europe,
Japan and the US. The result is an international AI investment
chain, with growth increasingly ﬂowing to the countries that hold
its critical links. It also explains a good part of the surge in the US
import bill: every data centre built in Texas shows up in Taiwan's

export statistics.
Trade policy is the second force reshaping the chain. Export controls
on advanced chips to China, tariﬀs and subsidy programmes such
as the US Chips Act (or direct government stakes in companies) are
pushing companies to build capacity closer to home or in friendly
countries. That makes the chain more resilient on paper, but also
more expensive – another reason why the deﬂationary promise of
AI may take longer to materialise.
The ﬂip side is concentration risk. If the chain snaps – through a

demand slump, an escalation of export controls or a Taiwan crisis –
the countries beneﬁting most today would feel it ﬁrst and hardest.
What does AI mean for labour markets, and
for how central banks read them?
So far, AI is reshaping who gets hired rather than how many are
employed – which makes it a real-time headache for central
banks.
Evidence on AI's labour market impact remains fragmented, and
the economy-wide eﬀect is still limited. If history is any guide, fears

of mass unemployment are overdone: with every major technology
of past centuries, growth was strong enough to create jobs
elsewhere, even as demand for some types of labour fell. But if
adoption is so fast that labour demand and supply can't adjust in
time, unemployment could rise temporarily.
The ﬁrst cracks are showing at the entry level. In the US, job growth
has remained positive but weaker in sectors with high AI exposure;
evidence from the eurozone is mixed. The Dallas Fed ﬁnds that

### Page 13

employment among young workers in AI-exposed occupations has
weakened, mainly reﬂecting lower hiring rather than more layoﬀs.
According to the Stanford Digital Economy Lab, employment
among 22-25-year-olds in highly exposed occupations was around
19% below where it would have been by June 2026 had it kept
pace with less-exposed occupations. In China, we see similar
pressures on entry-level work reﬂected in rising youth
unemployment. The Chinese government has planned training and

job replacement policies to help alleviate any negative eﬀects of AI
on employment. For developing Asia, particularly India and the
Philippines where outsourcing sectors are highly exposed to AI, the
evidence points to adaptation rather than decline. AI is changing
the nature of outsourced work, not reducing demand for it.
AI is not ﬁring people; it is quietly not hiring them. Still, none of this
points to mass displacement. Yet. And with ageing populations and

persistent labour shortages, the real question may be whether
shortages or AI turn out to be the bigger problem.
For central banks, AI is less of a short-run issue than you might
expect. Higher productivity growth combined with more muted job
growth could be the healthy sign of an economy absorbing a new
technology, and come with more moderate inﬂation. The tricky
part is reading it in real time. A soft payrolls print could signal either
a weakening economy or a more productive one – and the right

policy response would be very diﬀerent. Get it wrong, and a central
bank either cuts rates into a productivity boom or sits on its hands
as the economy weakens.
Who pays?
Is the boom still funded from cash ﬂows, or
are hyperscalers increasingly turning to
debt?
Still mostly from cash ﬂows – but the cushion is thinning and the
borrowing has begun.

### Page 14

The largest hyperscalers have so far paid for the AI build-out out of
their own pockets, which is the biggest diﬀerence from the dotcom
and railway booms. But capex is absorbing a rising share of
operating cash ﬂow, squeezing free cash ﬂow and leaving less room
for share buybacks. For companies that used to return cash to
shareholders at a record pace, that is a notable shift in priorities.
Borrowing is stepping up. Several hyperscalers, such as Amazon,

Alphabet, Meta, and Oracle have issued jumbo bond deals since
2025, with Oracle the most leveraged of the group. Equity markets
are being tapped too: several big tech ﬁrms announced plans to
raise equity in June. None of this is alarming on its own – these are
investment-grade companies with strong balance sheets – but it
marks a change of phase. Cash-funded booms are self-limiting:
they stop when the cash runs out. Debt-funded booms can run
further, and fall harder.

The key is to distinguish between the players. The cash-rich
hyperscalers can absorb a disappointment in AI returns; their core
businesses in advertising, cloud and software keep generating cash
regardless. The neoclouds and AI labs are a diﬀerent story. They
depend entirely on outside money – venture capital, private credit,
vendor ﬁnancing and strategic investors – and their business
models only work if revenue keeps growing spectacularly. They are

the most likely point of stress if ﬁnancing conditions tighten.
In between sits a growing grey zone of leases, joint ventures and
special purpose vehicles that keep debt oﬀ the hyperscalers' own
books.
The direction of travel is clear. The boom started as a self-funded
venture by the richest companies in the world. It is gradually
turning into a credit story.
Leases, SPVs, private credit: how opaque
has AI ﬁnancing become?
More opaque than the hyperscalers' balance sheets suggest – but

so far, the risk rests on some of the strongest borrowers in the
world.

### Page 15

A growing part of AI ﬁnancing is moving oﬀ balance sheet. Special
purpose vehicles and joint ventures, such as Meta's Hyperion data
centre ﬁnanced with Blue Owl, long-term leases and GPU-backed
loans allow companies to fund the build-out without the debt
appearing directly on their own books. Securitisation of data centre
assets through asset-backed securities and commercial mortgage-
backed securities is growing as well, bringing a wider pool of

investors into the game.
Leases are a particular blind spot. They are recognised as on-
balance-sheet commitments only once contracts are ﬁnal; until
then, the extent of incremental lease liabilities is uncertain. There is
also the question of who carries the risk if the tenant walks away
after the initial term. Although initial lease terms of 15 years are
not uncommon, these can still be shorter than the economic life of
the underlying infrastructure, leaving some residual risk around

renewal or re-leasing.
That said, risk has to be assessed contract by contract. The largest
technology ﬁrms, with very solid balance sheets, are well placed to
take on additional liabilities, and much of the oﬀ-balance-sheet
structure ultimately rests on their creditworthiness. The problem is
less hidden insolvency than hidden concentration: it is hard to see
how much of the system leans on the same handful of tenants.
Opacity also complicates the macro picture. When ﬁnancing is

spread across SPVs, private credit funds and securitisations, it
becomes harder for investors, regulators and central banks to see
where risks are concentrated – and harder to judge how a shock
would travel.
Looking ahead, capital may eventually become a major constraint
on the AI build-out, alongside permitting and access to power. The
more the industry relies on creative ﬁnancing, the more it depends
on investors' continued willingness to lend. Financial engineering

can prolong a boom, but it can't make the returns appear.
How exposed are banks, and is private
credit the shadow banking of this cycle?

### Page 16

Direct bank exposure looks modest; private credit is the obvious
weak spot – but contagion would run through markets rather than
bank failures.
Banks' direct exposure to AI looks limited. The hyperscalers fund
themselves mainly through cash ﬂow and bond markets rather
than bank loans. Indirect exposure is harder to gauge: banks lend to
private credit funds, data centre developers and construction ﬁrms.
Private credit is the obvious weak spot. Is it the shadow banking of

this cycle? Partly. It has less of the maturity mismatch that made
pre-2008 shadow banking so fragile: much of the capital is locked
up in closed-end funds, so investors can't run. But it shares other
features: opacity, infrequent valuation marks and leverage on
leverage, with funds borrowing from banks to lend to borrowers
that are themselves highly leveraged. And it is increasingly exposed
to AI through loans to neoclouds, GPU-backed ﬁnancing and data

centre developers.
The pressure point is the semi-liquid vehicles sold to retail and
wealth clients. They promise periodic liquidity on assets that are
anything but liquid. In a downturn, redemption requests could force
managers to impose gates, damaging conﬁdence across the asset
class. So far, these vehicles are not core to the AI build-out, but they
are where a shock could turn into a story.
If stress does materialise, we would expect contagion to run

through tighter ﬁnancial conditions and wider credit spreads across
the economy rather than through bank failures. That is less
dramatic than 2008, but not harmless: a repricing of credit risk
would raise funding costs far beyond tech, hitting the very non-tech
investment that is already struggling.
Banks may well escape an AI bust largely unscathed. The economy
they lend to may not.
Vendor ﬁnancing déjà vu: should we worry
when chipmakers fund their own

customers?

### Page 17

Worth watching, but this is not Lucent and Nortel all over again –
mainly because the balance sheets are much stronger.
The dotcom precedent is uncomfortable. Lucent, Nortel and Cisco
lent money to telecom start-ups so they could buy their
equipment. When the customers went bust, the suppliers took the
hit, and much of the reported demand turned out to have been
ﬁnanced by the sellers themselves.
Today, there are echoes. Chipmakers are taking stakes in AI labs

and neoclouds that then buy their chips. The same handful of ﬁrms
raise money, buy chips, lease compute and book revenue oﬀ one
another. The concern is less solvency than signal: circular deals
inﬂate reported demand and blur true end-demand. Revenue that
only exists because the supplier paid for it isn't demand.
But the picture diﬀers in important ways. Many of these contracts
appear to be normal commercial arrangements, and AI labs also
generate fast-growing revenues from customers outside these

related deals. AI-related revenues are still only part of the much
larger businesses of the hyperscalers. And the macro backdrop is
very diﬀerent: the overall US corporate debt-to-GDP ratio is not
worrisome, in stark contrast to the leverage that built up before the
dotcom bust and the global ﬁnancial crisis. Circularity in a cash-rich
system is a transparency problem; circularity in a leveraged system
is a solvency problem.
That does not mean investors can relax. Circular deals create

concentration risk: if one key AI lab stumbles, the shock would
travel quickly through its suppliers, landlords and investors, who are
often the same companies. And they make it harder to judge how
much genuine end-demand there is for AI – precisely the question
on which the whole boom rests.
The test is simple: follow the money until it reaches a customer
who isn't also an investor. The longer that trail gets, the more
nervous we should become.
Higher Fed rates helped end the dotcom

bubble. Could monetary policy or bond

### Page 18

yields pop this one too?
Probably not as the trigger – but monetary policy could make any
bust more painful, because the safety net is thinner than in 2001.
In 1999-2000, the Fed hiked rates to 6.5% before the dotcom
bubble burst, then cut by almost ﬁve percentage points in 2001.
This time, an energy shock is keeping inﬂation above target. Actual
rate hikes and more rate hikes to come could bring both the Federal
Reserve and European Central Bank closer to risky territory.

High policy rates do not necessarily stop AI investment ﬁrst. If
expected returns on AI remain very high, capital may keep ﬂowing
in while ﬁnancing becomes uneconomic for lower-return
investment elsewhere. Tight money could deepen the split between
tech and non-tech investment rather than end the boom.
At the moment, we expect hyperscaler funding needs to increase
further as investment in AI infrastructure, data centres and power
capacity accelerates. We forecast total issuance of around

US$280bn in 2027, including approximately US$190bn in USD and
US$65bn in EUR (€60bn). For European credit markets, we estimate
a net TMT supply number of €103bn, which includes US$65bn
coming from hyperscalers.
The long end of the yield curve matters more than the policy rate.
As the AI build-out becomes increasingly debt-funded, higher bond
yields and term premia raise the cost of ﬁnancing long-term
investments, from data centres to power plants. A sustained rise in

long-term yields would therefore hit hardest where the build-out
depends most on external funding.
The bigger issue is the safety net. If the bubble bursts, central banks
can't come to the rescue as aggressively as in 2001 while inﬂation
is above target. Fiscal space is also far more limited than a quarter
of a century ago.
But don't rule out a diﬀerent kind of rescue. AI is not just an
economic tool; it is also about power – geopolitical and
technological. If things went south for the sector, some form of

government support would be conceivable, from public

procurement to guarantees or targeted support for strategically
important companies. The dotcom bust was left to the market. An
AI bust might not be.
Monetary policy is unlikely to pop this bubble. But it could decide
how hard the landing is – and Washington may decide who gets a
parachute.
Bubble or not?
What do valuations and revenue gaps tell
us?
Bubble-like pockets, but not a bubble across the board: the leaders
are hugely proﬁtable, while the real uncertainty sits with the AI

labs and the revenue gap.
Valuations ﬁrst. At its 2000 peak, Cisco traded at well over 100
times earnings. Today’s AI leaders are expensive, but proﬁtable,
and analysts expect earnings growth to bring their multiples down
considerably. Microsoft’s P/E is expected to fall from 29.2x based on
ﬁscal 2026 earnings to 21.9x in 2028, Amazon’s from 28x based on
2025 earnings to 21x in 2027, and Alphabet’s from 20.9x to 17.9x
over the same period. With earnings estimates being higher than a

couple of months ago, that does not look particularly stretched.
The larger uncertainty sits with the AI labs, whose valuations follow
the venture capital playbook: spectacular revenue growth,
uncertain long-term economics.
Then the revenue gap. Capex is running ahead of AI revenues, as it
can take several years for investment to translate fully into
revenues. Amazon, Microsoft and Alphabet have invested around
US$600bn over 2023-25. As a rough rule of thumb, every dollar of

AI infrastructure may need to generate 40-50 cents of annual
revenue (assuming hyperscaler-like margins), implying US$240-
300bn a year – although not all of this capex was AI-related. That
looks large, but less so next to labour costs. At US$100,000 per
employee, US$300bn is the cost of three million workers. AI doesn't

### Page 20

need to replace them: a 20% productivity gain across 15 million
employees would deliver the same value. Consumers alone won't
close the gap; the money has to come from enterprises.
Two caveats. Concentration: the weight of the largest tech stocks in
the S&P 500 exceeds the dotcom peak, so any disappointment
would hit the whole index hard. And circularity in ﬁnancing and
revenues makes true end-demand harder to measure.
Finally, a bubble in prices doesn't mean the technology is a dud.

Railways had a bubble. So did the internet. Both still changed the
world.
What could end the boom prematurely:
disappointing demand, power bottlenecks, a
cheaper-model breakthrough, or Taiwan?
Most likely not a single trigger, but a slow squeeze: overcapacity,
eroding margins and tighter ﬁnancing if the revenue gap fails to
close.
Start with the usual suspects. Demand could disappoint if
enterprise adoption stalls and pilots fail to turn into proﬁts. Power

bottlenecks are more likely to cap the pace of the boom than to end
it, and could even stretch it out. A Taiwan crisis remains a low-
probability, extreme-impact event.
Cheaper models are the most intriguing risk. The DeepSeek shock of
January 2025 wiped almost US$600bn oﬀ Nvidia's market value in
a single day. Cheaper compute can raise demand – the Jevons
paradox – but higher volumes can come with lower margins. That
makes the proﬁtability of AI labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic the

big question mark. If it disappoints, the hardware will still be
needed, but the build-out would likely slow. Nvidia's exceptionally
high margins could also normalise if competition increases or the
scarcity value of leading-edge compute declines.
Missing from most lists is ﬁnancing: rising bond yields, a credit
event at a leveraged neocloud or private credit fund, or an energy
shock that keeps central banks hawkish.

### Page 21

Ultimately, we see the biggest risk in overcapacity. Continued
innovation in chips, more eﬃcient models and optimised
algorithms could eventually create an imbalance between supply
and demand, pushing down utilisation and returns. For now,
though, the industry is still struggling to scale compute and secure
enough power, while new business models and applications keep
emerging.
There is one more way to read today's capacity commitments: as
strategic entry deterrence. Announcing gigantic investment plans

discourages competitors from entering the market – and some of
those plans may never be fully executed if returns disappoint. That
would be a quieter ending than a crash. Not with a bang, but with a
series of downward revisions to capex guidance.
If tech stocks fall 30%, how much does the
wealth effect hit US consumption?
Less than you might think, but more than during the dotcom bust –
and the real damage would come through investment and jobs.
US equity wealth is highly concentrated. The top 20% of

households by income (earning more than $155k a year) hold
84.1% of equities, mutual funds and deﬁned-contribution pension
assets. That limits the wealth eﬀect. Fed research suggests the
marginal propensity to consume out of wealth fell from around 3.5
cents per dollar (1964-2011) to 2.6 cents in 2020.
A 30% stock market correction would wipe out roughly $24.5tn of
household wealth. At 2.6 cents per dollar, that means a drop in
consumer spending of around $640bn, or 2.9% of nominal

consumer spending (an annualised $22.3tn in August). With wealth
even more concentrated than in 2020, the multiplier is probably
weaker; at 2.2 cents, the hit would be around 2.4%. That is a level
eﬀect, typically playing out over one to two years – but a big
number nonetheless.
History oﬀers some comfort: during the dotcom bust, US consumer
spending barely ﬂinched. But it could be worse this time. First,
wealthy households are currently the only ones holding up
